One of the Showing Up issues that people are most interested in is relationships. In order to answer the question of how to apply mindfulness to our relationships, I’d like to start by introducing a few more elements of the

overall AQAL Integral Framework, starting with “quadrants,” and how they can be incorporated into a fully Integral Mindfulness practice, with special interest in relationships.

The 4 Quadrants

We earlier touched on the fact that the quadrants are a component of the AQAL Framework. “Quadrants” simply means the four fundamental dimensions or perspectives through which virtually anything can be viewed —and should be viewed if we want to make sure we are covering all the important bases with reference to any item we’re examining.

The point about these 4 fundamental perspectives is that each of them gives us very important, but very different, types of information and data. And yet very few approaches include all 4 of them. Rather, major schools in different disciplines all tend to focus on just one of these perspectives, leaving out the others or even denying their reality. Every quadrant has its intense champions; few approaches, alas, champion them all. Yet if we do so, we get a much richer, truer, fuller, more effective view of everything from government to business, from security to foreign policy, from science to art, from morals to history, from economics to law, from growth to therapy, from romantic relationships to child-rearing, from psychology to philosophy to spirituality. All of those topics—in fact, virtually any topic whatsoever—can be approached in an integral, inclusive, comprehensive fashion—including structures and states, and now “quadrants” (and soon, “lines”), and when we do so, the results are much, much more satisfying.

The Quadrants in Business

To give a quick introductory example of applying the Integral approach to a field, we can take the major theories of business management—how to manage employees, what management is, how it operates, and how to succeed in doing it best. Experts tend to agree that there are at least 4 major types of management theories prevalent today—known as Theory X, Theory Y, culture management, and systems theory.

Theory X focuses on the individual worker and the individual product in an objective, scientific, exterior, analytic fashion. It focuses on individual reward and punishment—the so-called “carrot and stick” motivation—and it

also gives attention to the individual product and quality-control measures. But the individual and its objective exteriors and behaviors are the major focus here. This is still a primary type of management in today’s business world.

Theory Y, on the other hand, looks at the interiors of the individual worker —at what makes employees (and leaders) happy; how they can find meaning in their work; how their jobs can provide value and purpose in their lives; how the workplace can become a source of joyful engagement. Maslow’s needs hierarchy is often pointed to in this regard. Maslow found that individuals grow and develop through a nested hierarchy of needs—in sequence, they are physiological needs, safety needs, belongingness needs, self-esteem needs, self-actualization needs, and self-transcendence needs (those are variations, of course, on our 6-to-8 major levels of development as they appear in the needs line). As a lower need emerges and is satisfied, then the next higher need can emerge; if that is adequately satisfied, then the next higher emerges, and so on. The point is that individuals have different needs and motivations at different stages of growth and development, and individuals at different levels of needs will be motivated to work for very different reasons, and hence as employees they need to be managed in very different ways. For example, individuals at self-actualization needs find that meaning in work is more important than what they are paid to work—and so meaning and value, not salary, become most important for them. The famous polling company Gallup conducted a worldwide survey, asking people what is most important for their happiness. The answer given most often was not money, not family or marriage, not fame—rather, it was “a good job”—work that is meaningful, purpose-driven, and valuable. As the chairman of Gallup, Inc., stated, “What the whole world wants is a good job. This is one of the most important discoveries Gallup has ever made.”1 And yet, studies consistently show that in the West, fewer than one-third of all employees are engaged in, or happy with, their jobs—that’s a horrible statistic. Well, that’s what Theory Y is about—the interior of individuals is the most important ingredient in excellent business management. Satisfy the interior, intrinsic needs and drives of the individual employee, and you will have a successful (and happy!) company.

The third major type of management theory is an entirely new approach that burst onto the scene in the 1980s, referred to as “culture management.” What is culture? Culture is, in a sense, the interior of a group: it’s the group’s

shared values, meanings, purpose, ethics and morals, mutual understanding, shared habits and history and worldviews. It’s what holds a group together from the inside (just as its exterior systems and networks hold it together from the outside). All individuals exist in many various groups—family, friends, colleagues, sometimes religious or political affiliations, tribes, states, nations, a collective humanity. And every business, as a particular group of individuals, has a specific culture, an interior set of values and meanings and rules and roles that hold the group together from within. As management experts began studying what matters most in business management, they soon hit upon the company’s culture itself. Professor James Heskett of Harvard Business School confirms that a strong culture can help or hurt performance. Effective culture accounted for “up to half \[Half!\] of the difference in operating profit” between two organizations in the same business, according to his research.2 Another expert put it, “Guiding culture is the single most significant job of any leader. There is culture, and there’s everything else.” Peter Drucker, world-famous leadership guru, is said to have asserted: “Culture eats strategy for breakfast”—in other words, guiding culture is more important than business strategy and planning itself! In short, culture management is managing the interiors of the group.

The fourth and final major theory is the ever-present school of systems theory. Where culture management looks at the interiors of the group—and finds them interwoven into networks of mutual meaning and understanding and values—systems theory examines the exteriors of the group—what the group looks like from the outside, in an objective stance—and finds them interwoven into networks of interrelated systems, structures, physical meshworks. Systems theory maintains that every individual is set in networks and systems of mutually interdependent and interwoven processes—and these systems, these networks, are what are ultimately real. Only by studying the whole system, and not its parts, can a true understanding of the situation be attained. And so business management and leadership that are focused on systems theory emphasize managing the overall system of the company as a single unified web (which itself is part of a larger market web, which is part of a larger system of international, environmental, and planetary webs)—and not as a collection of separate and atomistic individuals and divisions and parts. Now, systems theory does not deal with the major interior dimensions of individuals or groups: look in any textbook of systems theory, and you will find nothing on shared values, morals, art or aesthetics, purpose or meaning,

and so on. It deals with wholes, but only wholes as looked at from the outside in an objective, often “scientific” fashion. Systems theory, in other words, deals with the exteriors of groups.

So which one of those four is correct? According to Integral Theory and Practice, all of them are. So, following this Integral approach, instead of asking which one of those 4 is right and which are wrong, we ask instead, what view of reality would allow ALL of them to have an important piece of the overall puzzle, the overall truth of business management? After studying hundreds of different theories from premodern, modern, and postmodern times, Integral Theory suggests that, as indicated by business examples like the above, every phenomenon can be looked at through at least the four major perspectives or dimensions that we call quadrants—namely, the interior and the exterior of the individual and the group (or collective). This gives us four major perspectives or dimensions, the “4 quadrants”—the inside and the outside of the individual and the group—perspectives/dimensions that are intrinsic to the Kosmos at all levels, top to bottom.

Figure 3.1. Some details of the 4 Quadrants. (To view this figure in a larger format, visit www.shambhala.com/integralmeditation.)

As we will see, there are many, many ways these 4 quadrants can be represented. Figure 3.1 is one of these, and simply shows a few examples of the items found “in” each of the 4 quadrants, up to the leading edge of evolution today, namely, the turquoise Integral level. Notice that the same basic developmental levels—or levels of “altitude”—appear in all 4 quadrants, so that each item listed has correlates in all 4 quadrants/dimensions, highlighting the fact that the 4 quadrants are 4 different views or perspectives of the same fundamental occasion. But, looked at through the different quadrants/views, each item appears quite different and, in a certain sense, is quite different, since each perspective simply highlights a different dimension of the same event. The problem starts —as we will see with increasing clarity—when not all 4 dimensions are included in any account of any event; the result is a categorical distortion and denial of the reality of what’s actually happening. This is one of the primary contributing factors to the fractured, fragmented, broken, and shattered world in which most humans find themselves now living. And one of the simplest ways to begin healing this smashed and splintered world is by including the view from all 4 quadrants in any significant discussion of any important event.

Figure 3.2. Integral Business. (To view this figure in a larger format, visit www.shambhala.com/integralmeditation.)

Figure 3.2 is a simple summary of how we did this with Integral Business. And keep in mind that each of the quadrants also “contains” levels (also known as waves, stages, or structures), and lines (or streams), and states, and types. We have been focusing on structures and states in the Upper Left quadrant—the interior of the individual, or the “I” space (and the “I-I” space) —but these general areas (levels, lines, states, types) are present in all the quadrants. But there’s no reason to worry about these specifics right now; once we get a little better understanding of quadrants themselves, we will do some truly eye-opening Integral Mindfulness on the quadrants, which will dramatically make their reality vividly obvious, thus clarifying all of this merely “theoretical” discussion. We will particularly focus on personal relationships as examples, which is guaranteed to bring much clarity to any relationship you’re in—intimate, professional, familial, friendship. But first let’s look at the two quadrants dealing with the individual, in both its interior and exterior dimensions (or the upper two quadrants). The interior of an individual gives us an “I” space (home of Theory Y), and the exterior or objective view of an individual gives us an objective, outer, or “it” space (the object control of Theory X).

“I,” “We,” and “It”

So we will begin with the two quadrants dealing with the individual, in both its interior and exterior dimensions (or the upper two quadrants). As noted, the interior of an individual gives us an “I” space (home of Theory Y); the exterior or objective view of an individual gives us an objective, outer, or “it” space (the object control of Theory X).

Next we will look at the group or the collective, also in both its interior and exterior dimensions (or the lower two quadrants). The interior of the group or network gives us a collective “we” space (home of culture management); and the exterior of the group or collective gives us an “its” space (“its” being the plural of “it”)—a space of exterior collective systems or networks (the home of systems theory). But again, not just business but all activity—including relationships, we’ll see in a moment—can and should be looked at through all 4 of these quadrants. Clearly, all of them have something very important to

offer. These 4 quadrants—I, we, it, and its—are sometimes reduced to 3 major

quadrants, combining the two objective, exterior quadrants (it and its—the 2 Right-Hand quadrants) into a single objective “it” space—thus, I, we, and it. These 3 (or 4) dimensions show up in virtually all societies: premodern, modern, and postmodern. All major languages, for example, have 1st-, 2nd-, and 3rd-person pronouns. To review: 1st person is the person speaking (or I, me, mine)—the “I” space of the interior individual (the Upper Left quadrant); 2nd person is the person being spoken to (or you; and you + I = we)—the “we” space of the interior collective (or Lower Left quadrant); and 3rd person is the person or thing being spoken about (he, she, they, them, or simply it, or its)—the objective “it” space of the exterior or objective individual and collective (the Right-Hand quadrants).

These 3 quadrants are the basis of the qualities known as the Good, the True, and the Beautiful. The Good is ethics: how “we” treat each other (the “we” space). The True is what is objectively true, individually or collectively (the objective “it” space). And the Beautiful is the Beauty that is in the “eye”—and the “I”—of the beholder (the “I” space). As we saw with the 4 major business management theories, those 3 or 4 quadrants each have a different view or perspective of things, they have different values and meanings, they have different types of truth, and they have different types of information—all of which are deeply important.

The Quadrants in Relationships

How can I apply mindfulness to my relationships?

Now we have arrived at a question of great interest to most of us—how to look through all 4 quadrants at our personal relationships, particularly a romantic relationship. The first thing to realize is that every relationship has at least these 3 (or 4) major perspectives: there are two “I” spaces, coming together to create a “we” space, which is dependent upon various objective or true facts about all of those (or “it”) items. So we want to apply mindfulness to the “I” spaces; to the “we” space itself; and to any facts or “its” that apply in these cases. We have to lay some experiential groundwork for this relationship work, so again, please stick with me for at least the first few steps, and see if this process doesn’t enormously help clarify any relationship

that you might be in at this time. We can begin with a simple awareness of your own “I” space. Now, we

already did this when we looked at levels of development of the small, conventional self, or “I,” as well as when we looked at states of development, such as the Witness, or “I-I” (the “I-I” is, recall, Ramana Maharshi’s term for the Witness or True Self, since it is the Observing “I” that is aware of the small, conventional “I”). Here we mean whatever combination or complex of Ultimate Self (“I-I”) and relative self (“I”) that you might have. If you’re often aware of your Witnessing Self, and your conventional self is at, say, green, then your overall self-system is “Witnessing/green.” But simply, and generally, be aware of what your major state is (gross, subtle, causal, witnessing, or nondual unity) and what your major level or structure is (magic, mythic, rational, pluralistic, integral). This overall self (the combination of your structure-self and your state-self—the “dual center of gravity”) is what you will normally be aware of when you think of yourself or refer to yourself, and—the topic here—this overall self is what you have brought to this relationship, is what determines why you are in this relationship, what you want out of it, how much you are willing to compromise (or surrender what you take to be “true” in order to go with what your partner wants and takes to be true).

Now, one of the first things that many couples in relationship counseling are taught is to use “I” statements of personal experience or opinion instead of “it” statements that are allegedly “fact.” That is, instead of saying things like, “You are incredibly judgmental whenever we work together,” you say instead, “I feel incredibly judged by you whenever we work together.” That is your own experience or reaction; you are not accusing the other person of judgmentalism as an absolute fact, or blaming them with an objective “it” statement; you are simply expressing your “I” experience, which you own as your feeling, and you communicate it to your partner instead of calling him or her names. Clearly, “I” is distinct from “it.” “In my experience you seem X” is entirely different from “You are X.”

People don’t realize how often they mistake their feeling reactions—and also their intellectual opinions—for absolute, objective truth. Let’s continue with the example of opinions, which is simpler for bringing home our point. An argument almost always ensues when two people both treat their “I” opinions as facts (as “it” statements), and there is no way they can reach agreement if they keep insisting on the factual reality of their subjective

views. If one is right, the other is wrong—and they both think they’re right. They’ve confused their “I” and “it” perspectives (they’ve confused these two major quadrants). Whenever that happens, there is almost always a conflict in the “we” space as the two “I’s” clash over their versions of the truth, or each one’s “it,” both thinking they have the right version—the objectively correct “it” version. But actually, they are each just stating their own “I” preference, choice, or taste.

So let’s go into these perspectives, one at a time, with Integral Mindfulness, and see if we can’t begin to clarify this situation.

“I” and “It”

One of the first things you want to do in any relationship is get a good sense of the major differences between these 3 spaces—the “I” space, the “we” space, and the “it” space. So start with “I” and with “it.” Focus on a taste you might have—I prefer vanilla to chocolate milk shakes. Now, that I feeling —“I prefer vanilla, it tastes better to me”—is entirely different from claiming that “vanilla is the best” in an objective fashion, as if that were a universally true, objective, “it” statement. So focus on the feeling of preference—simply picture two objects—a vanilla shake and a chocolate shake will work fine, assuming you prefer one of them—and then apply mindfulness to the interior action you take when you look at both of them and then reach for your favorite. Preference involves a choice, and that choice involves your personal tastes. Be aware of all three—object, choice, and taste; see both objects in front of you (vanilla and chocolate shake); watch yourself ponder which to choose (replay in your mind what you do); then your preference arising—see yourself desiring whichever it is you like best; then the making of a choice and the reaching out for your favorite (and remember, you don’t have to do anything with those images—just see them, videotape them). As you do so, notice that the other option is still sitting there (as a part of the Total Painting of All That Is), and clearly get a sense of your choice being a taste, a 1st-person preference, not a statement of a 3rd-person “it” fact or universal truth. The other option is still sitting there, and many other people would be choosing that one.

Taste is an interesting phenomenon—among many possible choices, we prefer one, not for any universally true reasons, but for our own personal preferences. Personal desire is a key here (and desire is big in Buddhism; in

fact, according to Buddhism, all human suffering is caused by desire and grasping—we would say, by Primordial Avoidance); but what we can say for certain is that confusing the desires of the personal “I” with facts or objective truths (objective “its”) opens us to endless conflicts as we try to force our simple preferences and tastes on the world as if they were not choices but absolute givens that any person would and should make. These problems particularly flare up and become obvious in relationships, where, in addition to a series of facts about each other, we both have a wealth of opinions, tastes, and preferences tossed into the mix. If we have a conflict based on facts, there is usually some way to resolve that—we simply check a series of sources, or Google the items, and find out what the truth, what the facts, really are. You might be fighting over the actual cost of a house that you both just looked at, trying to decide whether to buy it or not. It’s simple enough to check—just look up the advertised price—and that ends that conflict. But whether you decide to buy the house or not—that depends, not just on objective facts about the house—its cost, its size, the number of rooms it has, its color, its location, the school systems and bus routes in the area, and so on, all of which are easily checked objective “its”—but you also both have your preferences, your likes and dislikes, your tastes. And that is where you have to be extra careful to recognize a taste as a taste, not a universal truth; it’s an “I” preference, not a given “it” fact.

So how do we feel when faced with facts, with “it” truths? Take any number of simple facts. Water boils at 212 degrees Fahrenheit. All bachelors are unmarried. A full moon exists, and occurs about once a month. What does it feel like to be faced with any of those facts? How do we actually react? There is very little desire or choice arising, is there? When you look at a full moon in terms of its existence, for example, as you look directly at it, there is just the isness of the moon, the thusness of it, its simple, undeniable existence, for you and all other people who care to look. No back-and-forth with trying to decide whether it’s so or not; no desires or preferences or tastes involved; just the simple isness of a given truth—there the moon is, it clearly exists, no complicated choices or preferences here. Now, to that “it” fact, I can add my “I” tastes and preferences: I might find the full moon romantic; I might find it silly; I might bring an astronomer’s fascination to it; I might believe it’s secretly inhabited by aliens. But one thing is for sure—there is a big difference between my “I” tastes and the real world of “it” facts, and nowhere does this become more obvious than in the right-or-wrong conflicts

between me and my romantic partner. Be as mindful as you can of these two very different domains or spaces.

Pick several items from each—from your “I” space preferences, and the “it” space of acknowledged facts—and see how you really respond to each when you are fully aware of which you are looking at. Pay particular attention to the big differences between these realms, and how really different they actually do feel, when given pure awareness. The “I” tastes always involve a choice, perhaps hidden, but present; but the “it” fact involves just a direct awareness of its “isness,” little choice about it. Pay careful attention as you switch from one to the other, and particularly examine the items you call “facts” and see how many of them actually involve your preferences or tastes.

Especially do this with any conflict or argument you have with your partner. Remember to use “I” statements instead of “it” statements wherever that is the more accurate view of what you are doing (e.g., “I feel strongly judged by you right now” versus “You are strongly judging me”).3 If you do so, innumerable arguments will simply cease, especially if your partner is reciprocating. You can then move from an irresolvable argument about “facts” (irresolvable because they aren’t really facts but tastes, and thus there is no source with which to check them, so that the argument, thus framed, can never be resolved)—and on to a discussion about compromising and jointly agreeing on how to combine different tastes into a resolution you can both accept. From “This is so!” versus “No, that is so!”—to “I prefer this” and “Well, I prefer that”—to “Well, perhaps we can both prefer to do it this way,” which is an active, mutually understanding way to deal with different “I” tastes in a particular “we” space, versus an irresolvable argument between supposedly “it” facts that, actually being tastes, can never be resolved.

“I,” “You,” and “We”

Now let’s shift into the “you” and “we” spaces. The feeling of “you” is particularly interesting. First, of course, it is definitely different from your own “I” space (although the assumption is made that a given “you” has his or her own “I” space, too). So take a few minutes to be mindful of these differences. Switch between being mindful of the feeling “I” and the feeling “you.” Notice how your “I” space feels in general (and that means how your conventional “I” space feels, combined with any ultimate “I-I” space you might have—your “overall self”). Basically, that means to notice your

separate-self feeling. Most people feel their “I” as being located somewhere in the body (the head, the heart, or the gut), so that might be one way for you to recognize your “I” space. The “I” is the home of innumerable particular desires, tastes, and preferences. But all of them are more or less under control of your self.

None of that is true of a “you” space, which is marked primarily by the feeling that this “you” space is not under your control at all. Your “I” space has its own center of control. But for various reasons you are attracted to this person, which basically means you are attracted to a bundle of their own “I,” “you,” and “it” characteristics: you like the way the person looks (their “it” facts—this height, this weight, this size, this shape, this smell, and so on) and their “I” personality traits. (Some personality traits might be universally perceived by most people—a demonstrable sense of humor, for example— but many of which you simply like as a taste or personal preference, just as many of their “it” facts you also like as a personal preference: some people like their partners tall, dark, and handsome, while others like them short and wiry; some like a muscular build, others prefer a Playboy centerfold’s figure, and so on through an endless list of personal “I” tastes.) But because of some odd combination of facts and preferences, you are attracted to this person (a fact that actually involves items from all 4 quadrants, from psychological factors in the “I” space to cultural factors in the “we” space to biological and hormonal factors in the “it” space).

But there’s a crucial value in being able to see, dialogue with, communicate with, and understand any “you” that you happen to meet, like them or not. You have to be able to take the role of other in order to have any sense of what the other person is thinking, feeling, meaning. And one of the most important factors in making relationships work is whether each partner actually pays attention to the view that their partner is taking. In other words, the capacity to set aside what one’s own “I” is thinking, and to see what the world looks like from the partner’s point of view, from the partner’s “I” space. The more you can do that—and the more your partner can do that with you—then the more your “I” and their “you” will combine into a “we” space, a space of shared and mutual understanding, the foundation of all genuine “we” spaces.

If you meet a person who has absolutely nothing in common with you— they speak a completely different language, you can’t understand a word they say, they’re from a totally different culture, with different values and

meanings and desires—this person isn’t even really a 2nd person for you (“2nd

person” is the person being spoken to), because you cannot speak with this person at all; in fact, they are not much more than a 3rd person “it” to you (“3rd person” is the person or thing you are speaking about)—in other words, a mere object, like a rock. And “absolutely nothing in common” means totally nothing in common—so no body language either. You can’t talk with them, you can only talk about them—in effect, there is no “we” formed at all, any more than you would form a “we” with a rock or a pile of dirt. So one of the hidden things about a “you” is that, if they are a real you (a person you are speaking or genuinely relating to), they are part of a “we,” or else you would never be able to see, talk, or communicate with them at all—they would be a mere 3rd-person object, a rock (their Left-Hand dimensions are not seen, only their Right-Hand dimensions—not a “thou,” but an “it”). The person might as well be in a coma or from Mars. They’re an “it,” not a “you.”

But this capacity to take the role of other, to take your partner’s view—that is, to attempt to see the world from their own “I” space—this is crucial to any good relationship. One of the most damning complaints you can hear in any romantic relationship is “My partner just doesn’t see me.” Their partner, in other words, is not authentically taking their role, seeing things the way they see them, understanding their own personal desires, their own preferences, their own tastes—and instead, the partner imposes their own tastes on the person. This usually means they have first confused their “I” tastes with universal truths or “it” facts, and then, since that’s the only way things could possibly be to any right-thinking person, they force those “facts” (their own hidden tastes) on their partner without even thinking about it. They take their own preferences as if they were all full moons—there for everybody to plainly see, the only way things can be, no discussion really needed, my tastes are the pure truth, that’s it. So my tastes must be your tastes, too—no need to discuss them, really. But on all occasions, even when people are faced with purely objective “it” truths—like a full moon—there is always the factor of how they react to, interpret, and feel about those facts. Does the person even like a full moon? Maybe something horrible once happened, conspicuously under a full moon, and they’ve hated full moons ever since. But when I confuse my own “I” preferences with universal “it” truths, I have no need to check and see what my partner thinks or feels or prefers—it’s got to be just what I prefer. And so the partner never feels seen, or heard, or understood, or ultimately, even loved. And that relationship is headed for the

drink—just like almost all of this person’s other relationships, until they learn to take, and value, the role of other, empathetically feeling into, and experiencing, their partners own “I” space.

So let’s do that right now. You’ve applied mindfulness to your own “I” space. You know what it looks like, what it feels like, where it’s located, what shape and color it is, what many of its fundamental desires and tastes and preferences are. Now do the same thing with a person you are in relationship with. Preferably your romantic partner, since that’s what we’re discussing now, but any partner you are in an ongoing relationship with— colleague at work, friend, family member. Start by being aware of them as an object, as a 3rd-person “it”—what they look like from the outside, what their features are, their overall size and shape, their height, their weight, their age —their “it” facts or features. And just notice them as “it” objects or facts, not whether you like them or not, desire them or not, want them or not—as Joe Friday on Dragnet used to say, “Just the facts, ma’am.”

Now imagine the last time you had a serious discussion with them (not an argument, but a neutral dialogue, although about a fairly significant issue). Be as mindful as you can, not just of what they were saying (although that’s an important start—what are they actually saying? Can you repeat it? Are you able to repeat it in a way your partner agrees is their intended meaning?). But try to also be mindful of what they meant, of what their view of the discussed material was, of how they were seeing it, what they actually thought of it, how they felt about it. In other words, be directly mindful of the role of the other—be as mindful as you can of their own “I” space. Put yourself in their shoes, and then practice mindfulness on what you see. Taking the role of other—the role of someone else’s “I” space—is almost never recommended in other approaches to mindfulness training, because there is such widespread non-awareness of the importance of all 4 quadrants. But this is a very important dimension, and deserves serious mindfulness, this “you” and “we” space, and it hinges on your being able to actually see and understand the other person—which means, see things the way that person him- or herself actually sees things. You see the difference, yes? To really see another person doesn’t so much mean to directly see the other person, but to see how the other person is seeing, what they are seeing. The other person is not just an objective “you/it” out there; they have their own “I” space, and that is what they want you to see and to understand. And that means setting aside your own “I” preferences, desires, and tastes—which itself depends upon seeing

them as “I” tastes and choices, not universal “it” truths, true for everybody. In setting aside your “I” preferences, you attempt to see the other person’s “I” space from within, as they see it themselves.

You will be better at this, the more you both share similar levels of development. One of the hard things about these levels, these hidden maps, is that because these maps are indeed hidden, we don’t see them, and we certainly don’t see them as maps. They are unconsciously taken to be the way things really are; they are unconsciously confused with the real territory itself. So the fundamental values of a hidden map aren’t seen as preferences or choices, they are seen as the way things are, for everybody, no question about it. These hidden “I” preferences, these hidden maps, are seen as universally true “it” facts.

So if you’re at orange and your partner is at green, you’ll be flabbergasted with each other. Your partner will be stunned at your drive for achievement and excellence, your constant drive for success, measuring yourself by your achievements (not your being), your wanting to stand out all the time and be better at things than anybody else. Your partner will keep saying things like, “Why can’t we all just get along together? Why can’t we do things that work for everybody? Why can’t we all be friends? Why all this nasty competition?” And you’ll keep saying, “I do not want to be in the same boat with those losers.” You’ll both think that you are seeing things the way they are; that you are looking directly at the real territory of utterly obvious and absolutely true things; you can’t imagine somebody not agreeing with these fundamental truths—it would be like somebody staring at the full moon and claiming it’s really a quarter moon.

But if you want a healthy relationship, practicing mindfulness on your partner’s “I” space should become a fundamental practice for you. Using Integral Theory or any good developmental map, you might have started to notice that you and your partner are actually at different levels of development, and you have started to get a sense of how your partner is seeing the world in different ways than you naturally see it at your level. If so, use that developmental map to select characteristics, traits, qualities, and values that your partner likely has, and look for those as you attempt to understand, and see from, his or her own “I” space.

But in any event, the more you adequately take the role of other—and practice mindfulness on that viewpoint—the more you do this, then the more your partner feels seen; the more you realize that your “I” perspective is just

your own preferences, tastes, and desires, and not everybody feels that way, they are not universal “it” facts, true for everybody; the more you can love others because of your differences, not in spite of them. And thus the healthier and healthier your “we” space will become, because your “I” space has made more and more room for the other “I” space in it.

The “We” Space

Now for a direct observation of the “we” space. First, get a general sense of your own “I” space and be mindful of that. Then shift to your partner’s “I” space—to a general sense of how you imagine they are feeling and some of the desires and preferences that might be arising in them right now. Look at the world through their “I” space for a few minutes, practicing mindfulness on whatever arises. Then back to your “I” space; and then, from your “I” space, put your attention on the specific feeling of “we” that you have with this person. This will be how your “I” experiences this “we.” Notice, among other things, that this “we” space doesn’t have what some philosophers call a “dominant monad.” That is, when my dog, for example, gets up and moves across the room, every atom, molecule, and cell in the dog gets up and moves right along with it. Half of them don’t go one way and half the other. This is not a democracy. No, they all get up and move right across the room with the dog’s “dominant monad.”

But no “we”—no group, no collective—has anything like a dominant monad, no completely ruling perspective such that, at its command, every member in the group gets up and totally follows the dominant monad 100%. Individuals have dominant monads, groups have dominant modes of discourse, or dominant modes of communication, or dominant modes of resonance, ways in which they communicate that all, or almost all, of the members can understand and resonate with. But no dominant monad. And that means the group isn’t a big organism, a single super-entity, with all of its members being cells in this big organism. Individuals in a group are not the same as atoms in a molecule, or molecules in a cell, or cells in an organism— they are not parts of a bigger “I”; they are members of a collective “we.” The “I” has a dominant monad; the “we” does not. The “I” is an individual holon; the “we” is a collective holon. Those are very different things.

So notice that this “we” isn’t a single super-organism, with a dominant will or monad—so you do not directly control it, nor are you controlled in all

ways by it—it has a life of its own. It’s made of shared values, shared understandings, a fair amount of shared preferences and shared tastes (this is probably one of the major things that attracted you to each other—your shared values and tastes), a mutual attraction, and some sort of history. You each see this “we” through your own “I,” and thus it will be, as all things are, a mixture of actual “it” facts (universally true facts about each of you— biological differences, hormonal differences, sexual components similar in all humans, and so on) combined with the preferences, tastes, and desires of both of you. This “we” space is likely the actual thing you are attracted to in each other. That is, you don’t love the other person per se, you love how you feel with this other person. You don’t directly love their “you” as much as you do this “we.”

So be mindful of this “we.” Start with your own “I,” and do several minutes of mindfulness on this “I” (this might flip you into your “I-I” or Witness, but that’s fine). Then shift into an awareness of this “we.” Notice that you don’t directly control it, not the way you control your own body. This “we,” indeed, has a life of its own. Every time the two of you come together, the thicker this “we” becomes, getting richer and richer with history, shared events, shared concerns, shared solutions—and several ongoing conflicts as you both try and get your “I” spaces aligned. But be mindful of this “we”—what does it look like, feel like, where is it located, what size is it, what shape is it, what color is it? If you presently are having a major argument or conflict, imagine this “we” space if the argument goes your way; then imagine it if it goes their way. Importantly, notice how clearly you differentiate your own “I” space from this “we” space. Go back and forth— from feeling your “I” space to feeling the “we” space—and see what differences you can notice. That is, do you imagine that your “I” preferences and tastes are automatically the “we’s” preferences and tastes, so that your wants are automatically what the “we” wants as well? (Which means you are not including the role of other, the role of your partner’s “I” space, in this overall “we.” Remember that “we” is 1st-person plural—which means two or more “I’s” have come together to form a “we,” not two or more “you’s” or “its.” So are you making real room for both “I’s,” or is your “I” the dominant component in this so-called “we”? If that is so, count on major, ongoing conflict in this relationship.)

“My We” and “Your We”

And now for an item that’s a bit more difficult, but a good indicator of how you are doing in being mindful of the role of other. You’ve already examined how your “I” experiences this “we.” And so far, virtually every time you have thought of this “we,” it has been how your “I” thinks of this “we.” What you call this “we,” in other words, is how your “I” experiences this “we.” But now you’re being asked to assume your partner’s “I” space. Imagine, as vividly and accurately as you can, some of the things that they are feeling, thinking, desiring—how they see the world. If you’ve never done this before, it’s going to be a real eye-opener for you—the fact that somebody could see things so differently from you, and that your “I” preferences and tastes aren’t universal “it” facts automatically and identically seen and experienced by all people, but are always a mixture of facts and interpretations. So do your best to experience the other person’s “I” space from their own perspective. Then, from their “I” space, switch to how you imagine they experience the “we” space. This can be a bit difficult, but with practice you should be able to eventually click very clearly with this perspective, this stance. (A lot of the initial difficulty is that so many people simply haven’t thought this way before, haven’t actually looked at the world through these different lenses and perspectives. They sort of vaguely knew that the other person’s various views were there, but never really looked into this, never really gave it any mindfulness.) Go back and forth several times, from their “I” to their “we,” being as absolutely mindful as you can be in each case, really videotaping each occasion carefully and clearly.

#### Now try a quick mindfulness run-through of all four stances:

Be mindful of your “I” space; then your experience of the “we” space; then the other person’s “I” space; then the other person’s experience of the “we” space.

So there are actually 4 “people” in this 2-person relationship! That is, the 4 major perspectives that actually come together whenever the 2 of you are together.

In all 4 cases, set aside a few minutes to focus on what is arising in that space, whether it is an “it” fact or an “I” preference (and if it’s an “I” preference, whose is it? Yours or your partner’s?). You might notice that

there are things that are genuine “we” preferences—things that you and your partner have agreed to agree on, even though it might be a compromise for both of your “I” spaces, and is thus something that doesn’t exist in either of your “I” spaces, but only when each “I” space experiences the “we” space, where the compromise is located. If a house that the both of you just bought was indeed a compromise between the two of you, be aware of that “we” preference, that “we” choice—it’s coming from neither of your “I’s,” but from your joint “we.” Be mindful of all of these “we” choices as they arise. Can you tell if they are predominantly and consistently related to one “I” more than the other “I”? If so, watch out. . . .

Then, carefully, move back and forth a few times between your “we” space (the “we” space experienced by your “I”) and their “we” space (the “we” space experienced by their “I,” as accurately as you can imagine it). Be mindful of both how you experience it and, as clearly and accurately as you can, how you imagine that they experience it. (And again notice, in each stance, whether what is arising is an “it” fact or an “I” preference, or possibly an actual “we” preference.) If you have fully differentiated and integrated all 4 quadrants in yourself, these perspectives will each be a distinct, clear impression.

This exercise of shifting perspectives is perhaps the most core practice you can do in any relationship. It’s absolutely crucial to bringing “I’s,” “you’s,” and “its” together in a functioning, healthy, happy “we” (with its own exterior “its” or system space—the functional fit of your particular relationship, along with this interior “we”).

Levels of “I” and “We”

Keep in mind one additional item that you might have to pay particular attention to—and this really separates the boys and girls from the men and women: if you are fairly certain that your partner is coming from a different level of development than you, then you need to directly, specifically, and deliberately take that into account. If you are at, say, turquoise Integral, and your partner is at orange Rational, then as you run through the 4 major perspectives we reviewed—your “I” experience and your “I’s” experience of the “we”; and your partner’s “I” experience and their “I’s” experience of the “we”—notice that it will be your turquoise “I” experience, and your turquoise “I’s” experience of the “we”; and your partner’s orange “I” experience, and

their orange “I’s” experience of the “we.” Further—and this will really stretch your capacities here—you will need to

at least try to fully reflect the actual differences between these levels, so that when you are mindful of your turquoise “I’s” experience of the “we,” that “we” itself will be largely a turquoise “we” (the “we” as seen by your turquoise “I”); and likewise, your partner’s orange “I’s” experience of the “we” will be an orange “I” experience of an orange “we.” You will need to do the best you can to vividly imagine what all of those are like. With regard to your partner, put yourself in what you understand to be the orange space (achievement, merit, excellence, progress, profit, drive), and then experience your partner’s “I” space through that lens. Next, experience especially your partner’s orange experience of what will amount to an orange “we”—and this means that they will be interpreting you in orange terms as well, so really spend some time with that. Go back and forth between how you imagine, as accurately as you can, what (a) their orange “I” experience is like, (b) their experience of an orange “we” is like, and (c) their orange “I’s” orange interpretation and experience of your turquoise “I” space as well. All of these are actual realities that are actually occurring—and your only option is either to be aware of them or to be ignorant and unconscious of them. A hard choice, but the only one we are given in actuality.

A central point, of course, is that your partner will not be able to directly or accurately see the turquoise space—nor, therefore, your turquoise “I” or your turquoise “we”—but they will be experiencing how those spaces look through an orange lens. Because of the inherent difficulties in such a disconnect, you can well imagine that individuals at different levels have an almost impossible time making a relationship work. If this is true for you, then there are likely many times that you have found your partner’s lack of a clear understanding of you to be very difficult to deal with. (As we’ll mention below, if you both happen to take up a similar path of Waking Up, this can help establish some commonalities across the levels divide, and give your relationship a leg up.)

But for various reasons, it appears that you are indeed in love with this person, and if so, although you cannot directly change how he or she sees you, you can start to take all of these different perspectives into account in your own case, and thus at least be able to meet this situation with an expanded understanding and awareness, thus easing much conflict. At the very least, you will be aware of the direct source of much conflict, instead of

being merely blindsided by it unconsciously, and probably lashing out in frustration.

And you can particularly use your understanding of the different major levels of development to attempt, whenever interacting with your partner, to fly at their altitude: in this case, to communicate in words that an orange level will more likely understand, and to interpret what an orange level says by understanding that they are indeed coming from that altitude, and that much of what they see and say will be determined by that altitude (just as much of what you see and say will be determined by yours). Now, this could be done in a very manipulative way—for example, by deliberately describing things that you want in orange terms so as to make them more attractive to your partner. Or it could be done in a very compassionate and caring way, using your understanding to genuinely try to communicate more clearly and understand more fully just what each other is saying. Ultimately, understanding different levels of development should be not a way to judge or rank people (that is always a misuse of the Integral approach); rather, it’s a way to increase communication and mutual understanding, extending care and concern and love in ever more effective and authentic ways.

One other point. For our example, we assumed that you were at turquoise and your partner at orange. It could be the other way around, yes? That is, they could be a level or two higher than you. And here you will really have your work cut out for you. First, you need to deeply understand that being identified with a particular level—and in this case, a “lower” one—is not a pernicious judgment; it’s certainly not a moral judgment or a judgment of worth. Remember, there are always higher levels of development available— endlessly—so everybody is at a “lower” level in some sense. And if you both were to take up a path of Waking Up, you might in fact make much more progress in that developmental axis than your partner. But the point is that different levels of development are simply functional, temporary stages on a never-ending path of ever-increasing depth and Wholeness, and each of us simply is where we are—with no judgment, no good and bad; it just is what it is.

Then, positively, your very attempts to understand a stage or two beyond your own center of gravity will be acting as a strong pull for you to actually grow and develop to those higher stages. The study of these models, remember, is psychoactive, and that is actually what you are doing, psychoactively activating the drive to these higher levels in yourself in a very

strong and straightforward way, so congratulations on that! But in any event, as you run through the major perspectives—your “I” and “we” and your partner’s “I” and “we”—simply (in this example) put yourself in your intellectual understanding of how turquoise views the world, and then view your partner’s “I” and “we” through that lens. You will be knowing this higher level by description, not by direct acquaintance; but again, it will have a psychoactive, consciousness-transforming impact on you, so do keep at it!

And keep this in mind: you have to be able to take your partner’s perspective before you can do any genuine work on the relationship at all. Thus, perspective-taking comes before learning particular skills, such as violence-free communication. You will find very few, if any, consultants or life coaches using all of these perspectives, because very few of them are fully aware of all 4 quadrants (let alone all levels and states). So use a tool like the AQAL Integral Framework to learn of all these various perspectives (quadrants, levels, lines, states, types) and do your very best to move forward fully.

A Spiritual “We”

Finally, you and your partner might be practicing the same spiritual path together. Sooner or later, almost any meditative or contemplative path will introduce a practice like mindfulness. The contemplative traditions are all about training awareness, and mindfulness is often a key component of that training.

Briefly, if the two of you are working together, this likely means that, sooner or later, one or both of you will fall into (a) a state of pure Witnessing mindfulness, also called the pure “I-I,” True Self, or core I AMness; and then perhaps, following that, (b) a state beyond even the True Self into a nondual Suchness, Thusness, or Isness, the pure headless, thoughtless, total awareness of unity consciousness—not just an unwavering Awareness of the Total Painting of All That Is, but a nondual oneness with the Total Painting of All That Is. In both of those cases, the small, conventional “I” will still arise and still be present—it’s just that you are no longer exclusively identified with it. You are identified with your own True Self or Real Self, or you are identified with the Supreme Identity, the oneness of your deepest being with Spirit itself and the entire universe at large in nondual Thusness.

But that True Self or nondual Suchness still has to express itself through a

relative vehicle, which means it will look through the Form of whatever conventional self you have—along with its hidden maps. We already saw that you can experience a headless, nondual unity state and still be at virtually any level of development (amber, orange, green, etc.). The same is true of the Real Self or pure Witness state—you can be in a state of pure, unqualifiable Witnessing, but you will still interpret that state and what it sees through whatever mental tools you happen to have available, and that means whatever hidden maps are present in your conventional self.

This is why you want to practice not just mindfulness, but Integral Mindfulness. As we’ve noted, mindfulness itself cannot see the hidden maps that individuals at each level of development follow faithfully and accurately without having any idea that they are doing so. You can reach Enlightenment or Awakening and you will still be interpreting that state through whatever hidden map is still present. But the Integral portion of Integral Mindfulness (that is, the part provided by Integral Theory, with its developmental component of levels) can make these maps conscious, can point them out, can make them “un-hidden,” and then regular mindfulness can laser that unconsciousness to dust—by turning those hidden subjects into aware objects, thus allowing you to dis-identify with them, thus making room for the next higher level of development, with its maps, to arise. Eventually, by making subject object and transforming to higher and higher levels, you will arrive at the very leading edge of evolution itself—which today is somewhere around the Integral level—and thus possess the broadest, most whole, most inclusive, most caring and loving and embracing level of development possible at this stage in evolution. When that highest level of Growing Up is added to the highest state of Waking Up—namely, nondual unity consciousness—the result is a full and complete Enlightenment, combining the best and brightest of both of these fundamental paths of human growth and development, resulting in what could, with little exaggeration, be called “super-human.” For the first time in history, these two fundamental flows of human development have been brought together, offering individuals an almost super-human potential for discovering their very deepest and highest dimensions of being.

When both you and your partner ground yourselves in either pure Witnessing (turiya) or ultimate nondual Suchness (turiyatita), you will vastly expand and deepen the basis of shared relationship between you—from a “conventional we” to a “Spiritual we.” You will also increase the likelihood

that, even if the two of you are at different levels of conventional development, your relationship might still succeed.

Martin Ucik, in his superb book written for men, Integral Relationships, uses the AQAL Framework to analyze relationships, especially when individuals are at different places in each of those fundamental areas. His extensive experience in the area has led him to the conclusion (which most developmentalists would share) that individuals who are at different areas in 4 of the 5 major AQAL elements (namely, quadrants, lines, states, and types) can still have a fairly productive relationship. But for those who are different in that 5th element—namely, who are at different levels (different structure-stages of Growing Up)—his only advice is: “Sorry.” He simply finds that levels are so different from each other—different truths, different needs, different desires, different values—that it is almost impossible for two partners at different levels to find enough mutual understanding and shared values to really stay together.

But we’ve noted that you can be at any level and have an experience of virtually any state—including Witnessing and Suchness. Therefore, even if you and your partner are at different levels of Growing Up, finding shared states in Waking Up can add a needed ground of commonality between you, making up for some of the dramatic differences and conflicts that dissimilar levels will throw into your relationship. In order to have a more balanced, coherent, joyous life, it’s important to be on a general path of Waking Up in any event, and if you and your partner can do so together, you will have added a profound source of shared material that can only deepen and broaden the romantic “we.”

The Quadrants and Showing Up

I’ve mentioned several times that not just relationships, but essentially all human activities and disciplines, can—and should—be looked at through all 4 quadrants or dimensions/perspectives that exist with any phenomena anywhere in the universe. Many philosophers and most Traditions maintain that a world comes into being when subject and object are differentiated from each other, thus introducing a perceiving element different from that which is perceived—creating a real world, in other words. Integral Theory agrees with that, but adds that another distinction is also needed to really get a universe up and running, and that is the “singular and plural” or “individual and

collective.” A single boundary—between subject and object—would just run on forever in both directions, with just one giant subject looking at one giant object. But both of those—subjects and objects—can exist in individual and in collective forms, hence breaking up the single boundary into innumerable ones, and thus giving us infinite fractals of repeating differentiations and integrations of all 4 resulting quadrants; and with that, a real universe is up and running, with real holons—wholes that are parts of larger wholes— beginning their relentless evolutionary unfolding and “creative advance into novelty.” Thus are real universes born.

But that does mean that everything in the manifest universe—from subatomic particles at one end to manifest Spirit at the other—has at least these 4 quadrants or dimensions/perspectives as part of its very makeup, its very being. And thus, if we want a genuinely inclusive and comprehensive view of any phenomenon at all, we want to approach it at the very least as it appears in all 4 quadrants. We saw how this works in business and management, and in personal relationships. The same is true of every other area of human (and nonhuman) activity. The problem, as previously noted, is that most human disciplines believe that only one (or two at most) of these quadrants/dimensions are really “real,” and hence devote themselves to promoting their favorite quadrant and trashing all the others. This is disastrous, just disastrous. You can probably get a sense, just from the business management example, of how unfortunate it would be to select only one of those theories and ignore all the others. The amount of data, of supporting evidence, for all 4 of these dimensions is overwhelming, just overwhelming, and each of them has some incredibly important truths to convey. Failing that, you’re just ignoring real areas of a real reality, and the results are always—always—much less than satisfactory.

Including an awareness of all 4 quadrants of any area that we wish to understand is simply a way to “Show Up” for the full reality of that area. By fully Showing Up, we open ourselves to all the areas where other items could also be occurring that we were tending to ignore. When we include the Upper Left quadrant, for example, we are more likely to be aware of stages of Growing Up, Waking Up, and Cleaning Up. When we include the Lower Left quadrant, we are much more sensitive to the distinctions in the ways different cultures and contexts view the world, value the world, and act toward the world. Thus, when we look at the Lower Left quadrant, the importance of context becomes very clear; the multicultural dimensions of all phenomena

become much more transparent; and the importance of the cultural background (and the intersubjective dimension) for individual personality formation becomes overwhelmingly obvious. Likewise, recognizing the Lower Right quadrant reminds us that matter is not just the “lowest level” in the Great Chain of Being (such as “matter, body, mind, soul, spirit”), but matter is also the actual exterior dimension of all of the levels in the Great Chain. Every Upper Left consciousness state, for example, has a correlate in Upper Right brain states; this does not mean that mind or consciousness can be reduced to brain—or vice versa—but that they are tetra-correlates—and all of these exteriors are interwoven, interconnected, dynamically interrelated in vast systems and networks of interobjective meshes (the Lower Right). Understanding these interwoven systems adds a crucial dimension to our understanding of virtually anything, because every single thing and event in the entire universe is part of numerous great systems and networked meshworks that are an inseparable aspect of its own existence (and ours). And, of course, hardly anybody forgets the Upper Right quadrant—the exterior of the individual object; and for many thinkers (from behaviorists to positivists), that is the only really real quadrant there is. For Integral Theory, the Upper Right is incredibly important—but only “one-fourth” of the overall story of a full Showing Up.

A 4-Quadrant Tour

The next time you hear somebody “explaining” something, listen carefully and see if you can’t spot which of the quadrants they are coming from. They will almost always be expressing a deep-seated belief in just one of the major quadrant-views. And simply realize that however “true but partial” what they are saying might be, it is definitely “partial.” They are explaining the world as if only that one quadrant, and that quadrant alone, were real, and all the others are implicitly or explicitly dismissed. (Of course, it’s likely they are ignoring any number of other items in the AQAL Integral Framework as well —leaving out structures/levels or states or lines, or various types \[to be discussed in the next chapter\]—but we’re here focusing on quadrants.) What a different—and complementary—story could be given to their account if they presented a fuller Showing Up!

Figure 3.3 is another simple representation of some aspects of the 4 quadrants, this time highlighting a few of the items that particularly relate to

human beings. This is a good diagram with which to summarize—and close —this chapter. You can see here some important correlations in all 4 quadrants. For example, the cultures labeled “premodern” in the Lower Left tended to arise in parallel with Magic, Magic-Mythic (egocentric), and Mythic structures in individuals (Upper Left); these correlated respectively with foraging, horticultural, and agrarian techno-economic modes in the Lower Right. “Modern” cultures in the Lower Left tended to arise with Rational structures in individuals (Upper Left) and with industrial modes in the Lower Right. “Postmodern” cultures tended to arise with Pluralistic structures in individuals (Upper Left) and informational networks (e.g., the Net) in the Lower Right. In the United States, for example, many of the agrarian states (in the South and Midwest) are still associated with a high percentage of fundamentalist, mythic Christianity; and the industrial and informational Northeast and far West correlate much more with modern and postmodern modes of culture. The “Culture Wars” are anchored not only in structures of consciousness (mythic, rational, and pluralistic) in the Upper Left, but in techno-economic modes of social interaction (agrarian, industrial, and informational) in the Lower Right. We are especially waiting to see what techno-economic mode in the Lower Right will accompany the Integral structures and cultures of the Left Hand quadrants. (My guess? Cyborg, or humans with direct computer/machine interfaces.) What we can take away from this discussion is the central idea that, as the essential ingredients in any manifest universe—what’s required to actually get a world up and running— all 4 quadrants are ever-present, a part of the Total Painting of All That Is, and a part it behooves us to be well aware of. Practicing awareness of (and mindfulness on) the quadrants will help clarify and delineate them in your Awareness—as well as help you identify with their ultimate or true Seer and Witness (which will further “fall into” a oneness with all of them in pure unity consciousness). The quadrants are 4 perspectives on the Total Painting of All That Is—4 different ways to look at the same Total Painting, or, if you will, 4 different aspects of the same Total Painting. When you impartially Witness the Total Painting of the World—or if you become One Taste with the Total Painting—make sure that you are Witnessing or becoming One with all 4 worlds. Any time that you are practicing a general open mindfulness, run images of all 4 quadrants through your mind, and make each of them a clear, distinct, obvious object of Awareness (just as you did with “I,” “we,” and “it” in your relationships). Otherwise, they will remain unfolded, hidden,

tucked into the corners of the Kosmos that your Awareness is not touching— even though they will continue to smash into you. Don’t let that happen unconsciously or in a hidden fashion, totally blindsiding you—make it aware, un-hidden, a clear object of your pure Subject or real Self (and Suchness).

Figure 3.3. The 4 Quadrants focused on humans. (To view this figure in a larger format, visit www.shambhala.com/integralmeditation.)

But what we’re seeing in all these areas—of Growing Up, Waking Up, Cleaning Up, and Showing Up—is that, while we never want to confuse map and territory, neither do we want a totally screwed-up map. And maps that leave out any of those areas—and every major map in the world, premodern to modern to postmodern, leaves out at least one and usually several of those areas—are maps that are about as screwed-up as you can get.

And yet humanity on the whole has, for its entire history, lived in some sort of less-than-Integral, less-than-inclusive, less-than-comprehensive

condition, codified in its considerably distorting maps, with all these various areas nonetheless directly impacting and smashing into them, while humans continued to make up causes and reasons for these impacts, causes that made sense at the time but, we can now see, are the thinnest and flimsiest of fabrications. Of course, cultures 500 years from now will look back at us as being hopelessly lost and naive, too. But in the meantime, let us take what Wholeness we can find—a little more Wholeness is better than a little less, and that is what an Integral view offers us: the most Wholeness we can possibly purchase in today’s world, and thus both the greatest Freedom and the greatest Fullness available to any sentient beings anywhere.

Jim Clifton, The Coming Jobs War (Gallup Press, 2011), p. 10. 2. See James Heskett, The Culture Cycle: How to Shape the Unseen Force That Transforms Performance (Upper Saddle River, NJ: Pearson FT Press, 2011), p. 2. 3. In Diane Hamilton’s book Everything Is Workable: A Zen Approach to Conflict Resolution (Boston: Shambhala Publications, 2013) she covers this topic of mindfulness in all sorts of relationships very thoroughly and helpfully.

Our Many Streams: Exploring Multiple Intelligences

We are on a quest for a fuller, more inclusive, more comprehensive view of the World—not because we are trying to complicate things, but because if the areas we discover are in fact there, then they have been impacting us, affecting us, influencing us, all this time, whether we knew it or not. We are not looking to be complicated; we are looking to be accurate, to be realistic, to be aware of what is already there, already active, already smashing into us, and thus making these areas increasingly conscious. That’s our real choice: not simple versus complicated, but unconscious versus conscious. I’m for conscious, aren’t you?

We’ve already seen the incredible significance of what happens when we become aware of, and thus include, stages of both Waking Up and Growing Up. Once you see the realities of both of those, it’s hard to imagine not being aware of them ever again. Cleaning Up and shadow material, although we didn’t dwell on it, is clearly an area that all of us need to be aware of. And we just saw the importance of looking at all 4 of the most basic distinctions that the universe makes as it comes into being (namely, the 4 quadrants)—less than that, and you have less than a full universe, and thus including all 4 quadrants is requisite to any full Showing Up.

There’s one last major area that is definitely worth noting, amid dozens and dozens—perhaps hundreds and hundreds—of still important, but definitely more minor issues. What we are trying to do with the AQAL Integral Approach is to find the fewest number of elements that cover the most amount of reality, and thus have a relatively simple, but hugely comprehensive and inclusive, map for navigating our way through this universe—a user’s guide to our own Kosmos, a Human Being 101.

And so for this last major area, I’ll introduce another very common question I’m asked about this approach.

Lines of Development

How should I apply Integral Mindfulness to my health, diet, and fitness; to

my career and money; to my family and parenting?

That “mega” question (and its many variations) is really asking, “How can I apply Integral Mindfulness to my overall life?” Whether it’s that general question—or any of its specific elements—the same basic answer is: Bring mindfulness to bear on every element of the Integral Framework as it appears in your life. We’ve already seen examples of how this works with levels, states, and quadrants. In each case, you very likely found an area or dimension of your own being that you didn’t know was there, and yet was playing a fairly profound role in your life—in your work, your relationships, your parenting, your play. By addressing these Integral elements—by bringing Growing Up, Waking Up, Showing Up (and Cleaning Up) into your life—you very well might have begun to feel fuller, freer, healthier, happier, and more aware, more caring, and more loving. Many of these quite numerous improvements start to occur because the elements of the Integral Framework are the very building blocks of a human life itself, and becoming aware of them—and Integrally Mindful of them—acts to supercharge them, to vitalize them, to profoundly activate and develop them. Not to mention what they can do for your own self-knowledge and self-understanding.

The same is true for the last major Integral element we will cover in this introduction to Integral Mindfulness—namely, lines (short for lines of development).

We saw that some major lines have already been made famous under the concept of multiple intelligences. It was long believed that we possessed just one form of intelligence—usually called cognitive intelligence and measured by what was considered the all-important IQ test. But for several decades now, researchers have understood that human beings actually have upwards of a dozen different multiple intelligences. Now, as different as these multiple intelligences or developmental lines are, they all grow and develop through those 6-to-8 major developmental levels that we previously outlined. So, different lines, same levels.

But the idea is that evolution itself has created, for each of the major areas in our life, an intelligence specifically designed to address that area— intellectual, emotional, moral, aesthetic, interpersonal, bodily, mathematical, musical, spatial, spiritual, and so on—so that we actually have (in addition to cognitive intelligence) emotional intelligence, moral intelligence, interpersonal intelligence, musical intelligence, aesthetic or artistic intelligence, bodily or kinesthetic intelligence, and so on. This discovery is

important for many reasons, as we’ll continue to see, but one of them is that, with the realization that all humans have access to a dozen or so multiple intelligences, individuals who might previously have been judged to be “mediocre” or even “very slow” in cognitive intelligence (and thus thought to be lacking in intelligence, period) could in fact be geniuses in interpersonal or aesthetic or kinesthetic (or some other) multiple intelligence—and this might include you! We have a vast store of unrecognized talent (and “geniuses”) in all these various lines, simply because—once again—not being aware of them, not including them in our overall maps, did not mean that they weren’t actually there, but meant that we simply weren’t acknowledging them, were not seeing them, and thus another major human potential was trashed and discarded out of simple ignorance. Well, no more . . .

Although researchers propose different numbers of these developmental lines, they generally agree that at least the following 8 are important.

Cognitive intelligence. Correctly understood, cognitive intelligence is not a dry, abstract, analytic capacity; it is the conscious capacity to take perspectives—1st person, 2nd person, 3rd person, 4th person, and so on—each is a more complex, more conscious, more whole, more unified level of awareness (or “cognition” in the broadest sense). This line is particularly important because it is necessary (but not sufficient) for most of the other lines—in order to be aware of your emotional intelligence, for example, you have to have an awareness that can do so in the first place—that is, you have to have consciousness or cognitive intelligence. So this line is foundational. It is often seen to be a stage or two ahead of most other lines (due to its “necessary but not sufficient” nature).

Emotional intelligence. Not just being aware of your emotional state, but also being aware of the emotional states of those around you, constitutes emotional intelligence. In other words, it has a strong interpersonal or social component to it—put simply, a taking of the role of other (dependent upon cognition, which again is “necessary but not sufficient” for this line). Emotional intelligence is thus how well you relate to your feeling states and the feeling states of those around you, and understanding how your feeling states interact with theirs.

The Pluralistic stage—also known as the level of the “sensitive self”—is fairly well-known for emphasizing “feelings” over “thinking” (which is actually often demonized). “Intellect” and especially things like “rationality” or “logic” are, for this stage, deeply suspect, and the emphasis is strongly on

“coming from the heart” and being “embodied” and “in touch with feelings.” This is why “emotional intelligence” (and feelings in general) rather than “cognitive intelligence” (and thinking) has been so highly celebrated. Orange critics, who are at least sensitive to the importance of reason in rising to worldcentric morals and evidence-based science, accuse the postmodernists of creating a “Republic of Feelings” and of replacing the First Amendment’s “freedom of speech” with “the right not to have your feelings hurt.”1

Whichever side of that debate you land on, we should at least remember that the depth that an emotion can reach actually depends upon the depth of cognitive development present. For example, in Carol Gilligan’s stages of female moral development—which, we saw, move from egocentric selfish to ethnocentric care to worldcentric universal care—each of those stages depends upon a higher capacity of cognitive perspective-taking (from 1st-person egocentric to 2nd-person ethnocentric to 3rd-person worldcentric), and thus the capacity to “be in touch with feelings” directly depends upon one’s capacity to “be in touch with intellect,” or one’s cognitive capacity (and thus, if your cognitive capacity delivers only a 1st-person perspective, then all of your emotions will be narcissistic and egocentric—not exactly something to be admired). You can’t even take the role of other—or really put yourself in somebody else’s shoes and see what they actually think, feel, and experience, and thus start to move from selfish to care—without a capacity for at least amber concrete operational thinking (also called the “rule/role mind” for its capacity to take roles and follow rules). Feelings can only be aware of themselves; to actually take the role of other, to put yourself in somebody else’s shoes, and see and feel the world as they see and feel it, is a cognitive act, a mental act. So if we come only from the heart, or from embodied feelings, all we’re doing is feeling our own feelings, narcissistically, egocentrically, selfishly. This is a very low level of emotional intelligence (red or lower). So we don’t want to shortchange the intellect as we try to “come from the heart,” but allow it to work in conjunction with feelings to provide deeper and wider and higher perspectives through which ever-greater emotions can expand and evolve. Again, cognitive intelligence is necessary but not sufficient for emotional intelligence. So, even if we value feelings above all else, and wish to “come from the embodied heart,” let’s remember not to trash intellect in the process—we’re just undercutting how deep our emotions and feelings can be in the first place.

Of course, one can overemphasize the cognitive line (although when
